Shuzhen Lv and Philip B. Zhang
1

Abstract




Joint Equidistributions of Mesh Patterns 123 and 321 with Symmetric and Minus-Antipodal Shadings

Abstract.

In this paper, we extend recent results by Lv and Kitaev by proving 20 (out of 22 possible) joint equidistributions of mesh patterns 123 and 321 with symmetric shadings, as well as all 36 joint equidistributions of these patterns with minus-antipodal shadings. Our results link several joint equidistributions of mesh patterns to various integer sequences, including unsigned Stirling numbers of the first kind, harmonic numbers, and the numbers of inversion sequences avoiding a certain vincular pattern studied by Lin and Yan.

Keywords:

Pattern avoidance,
Mesh pattern, Joint equidistribution, Symmetric shading, Minus-antipodal shading, Stirling number of the first kind

AMS Subject Classifications:
05A05, 05A15.

1
Introduction

The concept of a
mesh pattern
was introduced by Brändén and Claesson
[
4
]
. The study of mesh patterns has become a prominent area of research within the theory of patterns in permutations, which has attracted significant attention (see
[
9
]
). For enumerative results related to mesh patterns, see, for example,
[
2
,
7
,
10
,
12
,
13
,
15
,
18
]
.

An
n
-permutation is an arrangement of
\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, and the set of all
n
-permutations is denoted by
S_{n}
.
A mesh pattern of length
k
is a pair
(\tau,R)
, where
\tau\in S_{k}
and
R
is a subset of
{\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket}\times{\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket}
, where

{\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket}
denotes the interval of integers from
0
to
k
.
Let
({i,j})
be the box whose corners have coordinates
(i,j),(i,j+1),(i+1,j+1)
, and
(i+1,j)
, and let the horizontal lines (resp., vertical lines) represent the values (resp., positions) in the pattern. Mesh patterns can be drawn by shading the boxes in
R
. A mesh pattern with
\tau=123
and
R=\{({0,0}),({1,2}),({2,1}),({3,1})\}
is drawn as follows

A mesh pattern has a
symmetric shading

if the shading of the box
(i,j)
implies the shading of the box
(j,i)
, and

minus-antipodal shading
if, for any
i\neq j
, precisely one of the boxes
(i,j)
and
(j,i)
is shaded. See Table
1
(resp., Table
2
) for examples of symmetric (resp., minus-antipodal) shadings of mesh patterns.

Given a permutation
\pi=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n}\in S_{n}
, a subsequence
\pi^{\prime}=\pi_{i_{1}}\pi_{i_{2}}\cdots\pi_{i_{k}}
is an
occurrence
of a mesh pattern
p=(\tau,R)
in
\pi
if
(i)

\pi^{\prime}
is order-isomorphic to
\tau
, and
(ii)
the shaded regions defined by
R
contain no elements of
\pi
that are not in
\pi^{\prime}
.
Otherwise,
\pi
is said to avoid
p
, denoted by
\pi\in S_{n}(p)
.
For example, consider the mesh pattern
p=\bigl(123,\{(0,0),(1,2),(2,1),(3,1)\}\bigr)
.
The permutation
23154
contains two occurrences of
p
, namely
235
and
234
.
However, the permutation
14325
contains three occurrences of the pattern
123
, namely
145
,
135
and
125
, but it avoids
p
because some entries lie inside the shaded regions, as shown in Fig
1
.

Figure 1:
An example of the permutation
14325
avoiding
p
, where the circled dots in the diagrams represent the respective occurrences of the pattern
123
.

Two patterns
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
are
Wilf-equivalent
, denoted
q_{1}\sim q_{2}
, if
|S_{n}(q_{1})|=|S_{n}(q_{2})|
. Moreover,
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
are called
equidistributed
, denoted
q_{1}\sim_{d}q_{2}
,
if the number of permutations of length
n
with
k
occurrences of
q_{1}
is equal to that with
k
occurrences of
q_{2}
for any nonnegative integers
n
and
k
. The stronger notion of
joint equidistribution
of two patterns, denoted
q_{1}\sim_{jd}q_{2}
, means that the number of
n
-permutations with
k
occurrences of
q_{1}
and
\ell
occurrences of
q_{2}
is equal to that with
\ell
occurrences of
q_{1}
and
k
occurrences of
q_{2}
.

Hilmarsson et al.
[
7
]
initiated the systematic study of the avoidance of mesh patterns. This research was later extended by Kitaev and Zhang in
[
12
]
, focusing on the distribution of mesh patterns. Subsequently, Han and Zeng
[
6
]
resolved several distribution conjectures posed by Kitaev and Zhang. More recently, Lv and Kitaev
[
15
]
established 75 joint equidistribution results for the mesh patterns 123 and 132 with identical symmetric shadings, primarily using bijections.

Note that the permutations 123 and 321 remain invariant under the group-theoretic inverse, which corresponds to the reflection of the elements across the diagonal in the respective permutation matrices. This observation motivates our study of the joint equidistributions of mesh patterns 123 and 321 with certain shadings, extending the results of Lv and Kitaev.

In this paper, as suggested by computer experiments, we derive 56 of the 58 possible joint equidistributions of the mesh patterns 123 and 321 as presented in Tables
1
and
2
, which arise from 20 identical symmetric shadings and 36 minus-antipodal shadings considered in Sects.
2
and
3
, respectively. We also propose the remaining two pairs as conjectures.
Our main results are obtained through recurrence relations for the joint distribution generating functions, which lead to expressions that connect to several integer sequences.
The most interesting pairs, Nr. A17–A24 discussed in Theorem
4
, have joint distributions involving both the
unsigned Stirling numbers of the first kind
and the
harmonic numbers
.
It is also worth pointing out that one of the patterns in any pair among Nr. A25–A36 is equidistributed, as deduced in Corollary
8
, and is linked to the
inversion sequences
avoiding a certain
vincular pattern
.

This paper is organized as follows. In Sect.
2
, we consider 20 joint equidistributions with symmetric shadings, summarizing our results in Table
1
. The joint equidistribution of two pairs is left as a conjecture (see Conjecture
2
), which we confirm in the case of avoidance by proving the Wilf-equivalence of the pairs S21 and S22 (see Theorem
3
).

In Sect.
3
, we prove 36 joint equidistributions with minus-antipodal shadings and present our results in Table
2
. These results are connected to many known integer sequences, both with and without combinatorial interpretations, which naturally arise within our framework. In particular, the formula in (
8
) is related to the unsigned Stirling number of the first kind, while the formula in (
12
) corresponds to the harmonic numbers. Additionally, the formula in (
24
) is related to the number of inversion sequences avoiding a specific vincular pattern, as obtained by Lin and Yan
[
14
]
.

Throughout this paper, let
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
denote the mesh patterns
123
and
321
in question, respectively, with identical shadings. Also, let
\mathcal{T}(n,k,\ell)
be the set of all
n
-permutations containing exactly
k
occurrences of
q_{1}
and
\ell
occurrences of
q_{2}
, and let

T_{n,k,\ell}:=|\mathcal{T}(n,k,\ell)|.

Finally, denote the bivariate generating function

T_{n}(x,y)=\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-2}T_{n,k,\ell}\,x^{k}y^{\ell},

which enumerates the joint distribution of both pattern occurrences in
S_{n}
.

2
Symmetric shadings

In this section, we consider 20 jointly equidistributed pairs and two Wilf-equivalent pairs of patterns with symmetric shadings, as shown in Table
1
. In Sects.
2.1
and
2.2
, we obtain the joint equidistribution results by constructing bijections.
For each pair of patterns, we use
f
to denote the bijection that swaps the occurrences of the patterns in question.
In Sect.
2.3
, we get two jointly equidistributed results via recurrence relations.
In Sect.
2.4
, we conjecture two pairs of joint equidistribution and prove their Wilf-equivalence by establishing a bijection.

2.1
Results obtained by complement or reverse

In this subsection, we consider the following eight pairs:

S1 =

;

S2 =

;

S3 =

;

S4 =

;

S5 =

;

S6 =

;

S7 =

;

S8 =

.

Recall that the
complement, reverve, and inverse
of
\pi=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n}\in S_{n}
, are respectively defined as
\pi^{c}=(n+1-\pi_{1})(n+1-\pi_{2})\cdots(n+1-\pi_{n})
,
\pi^{r}=\pi_{n}\pi_{n-1}\cdots\pi_{1}
, and
\pi^{i}=\pi^{i}_{1}\pi^{i}_{2}\cdots\pi^{i}_{n}
such that
\pi^{i}_{j}=\pi_{m}
if
\pi_{j}=m
, for any
1\leq m\leq n
. Given a mesh pattern
(\tau,R)
, the corresponding operations are defined as

(\tau,R)^{c}=(\tau^{c},R^{c}),\hskip 14.22636pt(\tau,R)^{r}=(\tau^{r},R^{r}),\hskip 14.22636pt(\tau,R)^{i}=(\tau^{i},R^{i}),

where

R^{c}=\{(x,n-y):(x,y)\in R\},

R^{r}=\{(n-x,y):(x,y)\in R\},

R^{i}=\{(y,x):(x,y)\in R\}.

For instance, for the mesh pattern
q=(123,\{({0,0}),({1,2}),({2,1}),({3,1})\})
, the specific operations are detailed as follows:

Notice that the shadings of these eight pairs exhibit symmetry both horizontally and vertically.
This enables us to use the complement (or reverse) operation.
To be precise, we let
f(\pi)=\pi^{c}
(or
f(\pi)=\pi^{r}
) for any
\pi\in S_{n}
, which exchanges the occurrences of each pair of patterns.

2.2
Results obtained by swapping two elements

In this subsection, we study the following ten pairs:

Observe that for each of these 10 pairs, any permutation may contain occurrences of either
q_{1}
or
q_{2}
, but never both simultaneously. Moreover, by applying complement and reverse operations to the patterns,
Nr. S
9
, S
11
, S
13
, S
15
, and S
17
have the same joint equidistribution with Nr. S
10
, S
12
, S
14
, S
16
, and S
18
, respectively.
We shall prove the joint equidistributions of the first half of these patterns by swapping 1 and another element in permutations.

\bullet
For Nr. S9, the unique occurrence of the pattern
q_{1}
(resp.,
q_{2}
) occurs in
\pi\in S_{n}
if and only if
\pi=123\pi_{4}\cdots\pi_{n}
(resp.,
321\pi_{4}\cdots\pi_{n})
for
n\geq 3
; otherwise
\pi
avoids the patterns. Thus, let

f(\pi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}321\pi_{4}\cdots\pi_{n},&\text{ }\pi=123\pi_{4}\cdots\pi_{n},\\
123\pi_{4}\cdots\pi_{n},&\text{ }\pi=321\pi_{4}\cdots\pi_{n},\\
\pi,&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

\bullet
For Nr. S11, the unique occurrence of the pattern
q_{1}
(resp.,
q_{2}
) occurs in
\pi\in S_{n}
if and only if
\pi=12\pi_{3}\cdots\pi_{n-1}n
(resp.,
n2\pi_{3}\cdots\pi_{n-1}1)
for
n\geq 3
; otherwise,
\pi
avoids the patterns. Thus, let

f(\pi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}n2\pi_{3}\cdots\pi_{n-1}1,&\text{}\pi=12\pi_{3}\cdots\pi_{n-1}n,\\
12\pi_{3}\cdots\pi_{n-1}n,&\text{}\pi=n2\pi_{3}\cdots\pi_{n-1}1,\\
\pi,&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

\bullet
For Nr. S13, the pattern
q_{1}
(resp.,
q_{2}
) occurs in
\pi=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n}\in S_{n}
if and only if
\pi_{1}=1
and
\pi_{n}=n
(resp.,
\pi_{1}=n
and
\pi_{n}=1
). Under this condition, the number of occurrences of
q_{1}
or
q_{2}
in
\pi
is equal to the number of occurrences of the pattern

in the sequence
\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n-1}
.
Hence, we let

f(\pi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}n\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n-1}1,&\text{}\pi=1\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n-1}n,\\
1\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n-1}n,&\text{}\pi=n\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n-1}1,\\
\pi,&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

\bullet
For Nr. S15, the only difference with Nr. S13 is that the corresponding pattern in the sequence
\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n-1}
, which was

in Nr. S13, is replaced by

.
Thus, we adopt the same map
f
as in Nr. S13.

\bullet

Clearly,

for

and
\pi_{t}=1
for

f(\pi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\pi_{t}\cdots\pi_{i-1}1\pi_{t+1}\cdots\pi_{n},&\text{}\pi=1\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{t}\pi_{t+1}\cdots\pi_{n},\\
1\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{t-1}\pi_{1}\pi_{t+1}\cdots\pi_{n},&\text{}\pi=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{t-1}1\pi_{t+1}\cdots\pi_{n},\\

Recall that for a permutation
\pi=\pi_{1}\cdots\pi_{n}\in S_{n}
, a letter
\pi_{i}
is said to be a
left-to-right minimum
if
\pi_{i}<\mbox{min}(\pi_{1}\cdots\pi_{i-1})
. In particular,
\pi_{1}
is always a left-to-right minimum in
\pi
. For instance, in the permutation
\pi=45123
, elements 4 and 1 are left-to-right minima. We label the slots from left to right with the
(i+1)
-th slot located between
\pi_{i}
and
\pi_{i+1}
. In particular, the first slot (resp., the last slot) refers to the position immediately before the first entry (resp., after the last entry). Denote

T^{(1)}_{n,k,\ell}:=\#\{\pi\in\mathcal{A}(n,k,\ell):\pi_{1}>\pi_{2}\},

T^{(2)}_{n,k,\ell}:=\#\{\pi\in\mathcal{A}(n,k,\ell):\pi_{1}<\pi_{2}\}.

It is clear that

\displaystyle T_{n,k,\ell}=T^{(1)}_{n,k,\ell}+T^{(2)}_{n,k,\ell}.

(1)

To prove
T_{n,k,\ell}=T_{n,\ell,k}
, we shall show

T^{(1)}_{n,k,\ell}=T^{(2)}_{n,\ell,k}\text{ and }T^{(2)}_{n,k,\ell}=T^{(1)}_{n,\ell,k}.

We claim that

\displaystyle T^{(1)}_{n,k,\ell}

\displaystyle=(n-2)T^{(1)}_{n-1,k,\ell}+T^{(1)}_{n-1,k,\ell-1}+T^{(2)}_{n-1,k,\ell},

(2)

\displaystyle T^{(2)}_{n,k,\ell}

\displaystyle=T^{(1)}_{n-1,k,\ell}+(n-2)T^{(2)}_{n-1,k,\ell}+T^{(2)}_{n-1,k-1,\ell}.

(3)

As shown on the left side of Fig.
3
,
x_{i}ab
is an occurrence of
q_{1}
, where the first element is a left-to-right minimum and the second and third elements are in the area

A_{i}^{\prime}s
, which has been observed in
[
15
, Figure 1]
.
Referring to the right side of Fig.
3
, any occurrence of
q_{2}
consists of three consecutive left-to-right minima with
B_{i}=\emptyset
, as exemplified by
x_{i}x_{i+1}x_{i+2}
.

To generate a permutation
\pi=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n}\in S_{n}
counted by the left-hand side of the two equations, we consider inserting the largest element
n
into some
\pi^{\prime}=\pi^{\prime}_{1}\pi^{\prime}_{2}\cdots\pi^{\prime}_{n}\in S_{n-1}
. The items in (
2
) are as follows:

•

\pi^{\prime}_{1}>\pi^{\prime}_{2}
. Then inserting
n
in any slot except the second results in
\pi_{1}>\pi_{2}
. Namely,
in any of the last
(n-2)
slots, the number of occurrences of both patterns would be preserved, which is counted by
(n-2)T^{(1)}_{n-1,k,\ell}
. However, inserting
n
in the first slot establishes
n
as a left-to-right minimum with
B_{1}=\emptyset
, thereby creating an extra occurrence of
q_{2}
in the form of
n\pi_{2}\pi_{3}
, without affecting other occurrences. Thus, this case contributes to
T^{(1)}_{n-1,k,\ell-1}
.

•

\pi^{\prime}_{1}<\pi^{\prime}_{2}
. Then inserting
n
only in the first slot makes
\pi_{1}>\pi_{2}
. This insertion does not affect any occurrence of either pattern and gives
T^{(2)}_{n-1,k,\ell}
.

For (
3
), we divide our discussion into the following cases.

•

\pi^{\prime}_{1}>\pi^{\prime}_{2}
. Then inserting
n
only in the second slot makes
\pi_{1}<\pi_{2}
, and this insertion does not change the number of occurrences of either pattern. Therefore, this case is given by
T^{(1)}_{n-1,k,\ell}
.

•

\pi^{\prime}_{1}<\pi^{\prime}_{2}
. Then inserting
n
in any slot except the first ensures
\pi_{1}<\pi_{2}
. Namely, in any of the second or the last
(n-3)
slots, the number of occurrences of both patterns remains unchanged, which contributes to
(n-2)T^{(2)}_{n-1,k,\ell}
.
Inserting
n
in the third slot leads to
\pi_{2},n\in A_{1}
, thereby creating an occurrence of
q_{1}
in the form of
\pi_{1}\pi_{2}n
, without affecting other occurrences of either pattern. Thus, this case contributes to
T^{(2)}_{n-1,k-1,\ell}
.

The initial conditions are
T^{(1)}_{2,0,0}=T^{(2)}_{2,0,0}=1
,
T^{(1)}_{3,0,1}=T^{(2)}_{3,1,0}=1
and
T^{(1)}_{3,1,0}=T^{(2)}_{3,0,1}=0
.
By directly using (
2
) and (
3
), and applying induction on
n
,
k
and
\ell
, we obtain the results. Thus, we complete the proof.
∎

2.4
Wilf-equivalence of Nr. S21 and S22

The computer evidence also suggests two additional pairs of patterns with symmetric shadings.

Conjecture 2
.

(Nr. S21 and S22) .

The patterns in pair Nr. S22 can be obtained from those of Nr. S21 by applying
the reverse and complement operations. Therefore, it suffices to prove the
joint equidistribution for Nr. S21.
The Wilf-equivalence of the patterns in Conjecture
2
holds.

Theorem 3
.

We establish a bijection
f
that maps
S_{n}(q_{1})
to
S_{n}(q_{2})
as follows: If
\pi\in S_{n}(q_{1})\cap S_{n}(q_{2})
, then let
f(\pi)=\pi
. If
\pi\in S_{n}(q_{1})
and contains at least one occurrence of
q_{2}
, then we apply the following swapping operation.

•

Find the lexicographically first occurrence of
q_{2}
and swap the first and third elements of it, as shown in Fig.
4
. It is clear the occurrence of
q_{2}
turns to be an occurrence of
q_{1}
.

Repeating the swapping operations until there are no more occurrences of
q_{2}
, we obtain a sequence of permutations
\pi=\pi^{(0)},\pi^{(1)},\dots,\pi^{(j)}
, we let
f(\pi)=\pi^{(j)}
.

A

C
D
E
F
c
b

Figure 4:
The swapping operation that maps
\pi^{(i)}
with the lexicographically first occurrence of
q_{2}
(on the left side) to
\pi^{(i+1)}
with one occurrence of
q_{1}
(on the right side) in Theorem
3
.

f
is well-defined.

We shall prove the sequence described above terminates in finite steps.
Let
abc
be the lexicographically first occurrence of
q_{2}
in
\pi^{(i)}
as shown on the left side of Fig.
4
.
We claim that no new occurrence of
q_{2}
is created in
\pi^{(i+1)}
that is lexicographically smaller than the occurrence
abc
.
Suppose, contrary to our claim, that
a^{\prime}b^{\prime}c^{\prime}
is such an occurrence of
q_{2}
in
\pi^{(i+1)}
. Neither a nor b can appear in
a^{\prime}b^{\prime}c^{\prime}
, since otherwise
a^{\prime}b^{\prime}c^{\prime}
would not be an occurrence of
q_{2}
due to the existence of
c
.

•

If
c=a^{\prime}
, then
b^{\prime}
and
c^{\prime}
appear in the area
F
, which would force
cb^{\prime}c^{\prime}
to be the right of
abc
, contrary to the assumption that
cb^{\prime}c^{\prime}
is lexicographically smaller than the occurrence
abc
.

•

If
c=b^{\prime}
, then
a^{\prime}
must be in area
A
and
c^{\prime}
must be in area
F
. Therefore,
a^{\prime}cc^{\prime}
would not be an occurrence of
q_{2}
since
a
and
b
are in shaded areas.

•

If
c=c^{\prime}
, then the elements
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
are in the area
A
.
In this case,
a^{\prime}b^{\prime}a
would be an occurrence of
q_{2}
to the left of
abc
in
\pi^{(i)}
, which is a contradiction.

This proves our claim. Thus
f(\pi)=\pi^{(j)}\in S_{n}(q_{2})
.

f
is reversible.
We consider the inverse mapping
f^{-1}
. For any
\sigma\in S_{n}(q_{1})\cap S_{n}(q_{2})
, we let
f^{-1}(\sigma)=\sigma
. For
\sigma\in S_{n}(q_{2})
that contains occurrences of
q_{1}
, find the lexicographically first
q_{1}
and swap the first and third elements of it. Along similar lines, we can see that the procedure never creates any new occurrences of
q_{1}
that are lexicographically smaller than the lexicographically first one.
∎

. In Sect.

, we obtain the results by complement, reverse, and inverse operations. In Sect.
3.2
, we prove the results via recurrence relations.

A36 =

.

Recall that the
unsigned Stirling number of the first kind
(
[
16
, A132393]
) is defined by
c(n,k)=0
if
n<k
or
k=0
, except
c(0,0)=1
, and

c(n,k)=(n-1)c(n-1,k)+c(n-1,k-1).

Theorem 4
.

(Nr. A17 to A24). Moreover, for
n\geq 2
, all the pairs satisfy

\displaystyle T_{n,k,\ell}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}\binom{k+\ell+2}{k+1}c(n-1,k+\ell+2),&\text{}k\geq 1,\ell\geq 1\\[6.0pt]
(k+2)c(n-1,k+2)+c(n-1,k+1),&\text{}k\geq 1,\ell=0,\\[6.0pt]
(\ell+2)c(n-1,\ell+2)+c(n-1,\ell+1),&\text{}\ell\geq 1,k=0,\\[6.0pt]
2(n-2)!\,(1+\sum_{i=1}^{n-2}\frac{1}{i}),&\text{}k=\ell=0.\end{array}\right.

Equivalently,

\displaystyle T_{n}(x,y)=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n-2}c(n-1,i+1)(x^{i}+y^{i})

\displaystyle\ +\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-2}\binom{k+\ell+2}{k+1}c(n-1,k+\ell+2)\,x^{k}y^{\ell}.

(8)

Instead of directly finding the recurrence relations of any pair of patterns, we first consider two equidistributed patterns of length two by removing one row and one column that contains the first element of Nr. A17.

. First,
to prove

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}_{n,k}=c(n,k+1),

(9)

we shall show

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}_{n,k}=(n-1)\widetilde{T}_{n-1,k}+\widetilde{T}_{n-1,k-1},

(10)

together with
\widetilde{T}_{1,0}=c(1,1)=1

holds for both patterns. We obtain each permutation counted by the left side of (
10
) by inserting the largest element
n
into an
(n-1)
-permutation.

•

For the pattern

, the insertion of
n
in the last slot creates an additional occurrence of the pattern with
1n
, which contributes to

\widetilde{T}_{n-1,k-1}
. In any other slot, the insertion preserves the number of occurrences, which is counted by

(n-1)\widetilde{T}_{n-1,k}
.

•

For the pattern

, the descriptions for the terms in (
10
) are the same as those for

, except for the extra occurrence of

when considering
\widetilde{T}_{n-1,k-1}
, which corresponds to
(n-1)n
.

This completes the proof.
∎

The first element in each occurrence of the two patterns in a permutation
\pi=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n}
must be the first element
\pi_{1}
. We define
A=\{\pi_{j}:\pi_{j}>\pi_{1}\}
and
B=\{\pi_{j}:\pi_{j}<\pi_{1}\}
.
For each permutation
\pi
with
k
occurrences of
q_{1}
and
\ell
occurrences of
q_{2}
, the permutation formed by
A
contains
k
occurrences of

, and the permutation formed by
B
contains
\ell
occurrences of

.
By Lemma
5
, we have

\displaystyle T_{n,k,\ell}=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\binom{n-1}{i}\,\widetilde{T}_{i,k}\,\widetilde{T}_{n-i-1,\ell}.

(11)

It follows that
T_{n,k,\ell}=T_{n,\ell,k}
. This proves the joint equidistribution of
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
.

Moreover, (
8
) is obtained from (
11
) as follows:

\displaystyle T_{n}(x,y)=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-2}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\binom{n-1}{i}\,\widetilde{T}_{i,k}\,\widetilde{T}_{n-i-1,\ell}x^{k}y^{\ell}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-2}\widetilde{T}_{0,0}\,c(n-1,\ell+1)y^{\ell}+\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}c(n-1,k+1)\widetilde{T}_{0,0}\,x^{k}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}\,\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-2}\,\sum_{i=1}^{n-2}\binom{n-1}{i}\,c(i,k+1)c(n-i-1,\ell+1)x^{k}y^{\ell}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n-2}c(n-1,i+1)(x^{i}+y^{i})\,+\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}\,\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-2}\,\sum_{i=k+1}^{n-2-\ell}\binom{n-1}{i}c(i,k+1)\,c(n-i-1,\ell+1)x^{k}y^{\ell}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n-2}c(n-1,i+1)(x^{i}+y^{i})\,+\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-2}\binom{k+\ell+2}{k+1}c(n-1,k+\ell+2)\,x^{k}y^{\ell}.

The three terms in the second equality correspond to the cases
i=0
,
i=n-1
, and
i=1,\ldots,n-2
, respectively. The last equality follows from a variation of the Chu-Vandermonde identity

\sum_{i=m-r}^{n-r}c(i,m-r)c(n-i,r)=\binom{m}{r}c(n,m),

see
[
3
]
or
[
19
, Equation (17)]
.
For (
4
), by taking the coefficients of both sides of (
8
), we can directly obtain the number of
T_{n,k,\ell}
for
k+\ell\neq 0
. In particular, for
k=\ell=0
, we have

\displaystyle T_{n,0,0}=

\displaystyle\binom{2}{1}\,c(n-1,2)+2c(n-1,1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2(n-2)!\sum_{i=1}^{n-2}\frac{1}{i}+2(n-2)!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2(n-2)!\,(1+\sum_{i=1}^{n-2}\frac{1}{i}),

(12)

where
c(n,2)=(n-1)!\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{1}{i}
, see
[
17
, Exercise 1.43]
.
Therefore, we complete the proof.
∎

Remark
.

The number of
n
-permutations that avoid both
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
is
T_{n,0,0}=2a_{n-2}
, where
a_{n}=n!\,(1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{i})
is the
n
-harmonic number that appears in OEIS
[
16
, A000774]
, see also
[
11
]
.

In the subsequent two theorems, we establish the recurrence relation by considering three cases concerning the position of the largest element. Subsequently, we employ the notation

\displaystyle T^{(1)}_{n,k,\ell}:

\displaystyle=\#\{\pi\in\mathcal{T}(n,k,\ell):\pi_{1}=n\},

\displaystyle T^{(2)}_{n,k,\ell}:

\displaystyle=\#\{\pi\in\mathcal{T}(n,k,\ell):\pi_{n}=n\},

\displaystyle T^{(3)}_{n,k,\ell}:

\displaystyle=\#\{\pi\in\mathcal{T}(n,k,\ell):\pi_{i}=n,i\neq 1,n\}.

It is clear that

\displaystyle T_{n,k,\ell}=T^{(1)}_{n,k,\ell}+T^{(2)}_{n,k,\ell}+T^{(3)}_{n,k,\ell}.

(13)

.

To prove
T_{n,k,\ell}=T_{n,\ell,k}
, we shall show

T^{(1)}_{n,k,\ell}=T^{(2)}_{n,\ell,k},\hskip 14.22636ptT^{(2)}_{n,k,\ell}=T^{(1)}_{n,\ell,k},\hskip 14.22636ptT^{(3)}_{n,k,\ell}=T^{(3)}_{n,\ell,k}.

We claim that

\displaystyle T^{(1)}_{n,k,\ell}

\displaystyle=T^{(1)}_{n-1,k,\ell-1}+T^{(2)}_{n-1,k,\ell}+T^{(3)}_{n-1,k,\ell-1},

(14)

\displaystyle T^{(2)}_{n,k,\ell}

\displaystyle=T^{(1)}_{n-1,k,\ell}+T^{(2)}_{n-1,k-1,\ell}+T^{(3)}_{n-1,k-1,\ell},

(15)

\displaystyle T^{(3)}_{n,k,\ell}

\displaystyle=(n-2)T_{n-1,k,\ell}.

(16)

The above three equations can be proved by considering the ways of getting a permutation
\pi=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n}
from some
\pi^{\prime}=\pi^{\prime}_{1}\pi^{\prime}_{2}\cdots\pi^{\prime}_{n-1}\in S_{n-1}
by inserting the largest element
n
into a slot.

•

For (
14
), in each case,
n
is inserted into the first slot. The situation where
\pi^{\prime}_{1}=n-1
or
\pi^{\prime}_{i}=n-1
, an extra occurrence of
q_{2}
would be created, in the form of
n\pi_{j}\pi_{n}
, where
\pi_{j}
is the leftmost element larger than
\pi_{n}
and distinct from
n
. Thus, the two cases give
T^{(1)}_{n-1,k,\ell-1}
and
T^{(3)}_{n-1,k,\ell-1}
, respectively. The situation where
\pi^{\prime}_{n-1}=n-1
preserves the number of both patterns, which contributes to
T^{(2)}_{n-1,k,\ell}
.

•

For (
15
),
n
is inserted into the last slot in each case. First, when
\pi^{\prime}_{1}=n-1
, the insertion preserves the number of occurrences of both patterns, which gives
T^{(1)}_{n-1,k,\ell}
. Other situations result in an extra occurrence of
q_{1}
, which is formed by
\pi_{1}\pi_{j}n
, where
\pi_{j}
is the leftmost element larger than
\pi_{1}
. Thus, these cases contribute to
T^{(2)}_{n-1,k-1,\ell}+T^{(3)}_{n-1,k-1,\ell}
. Summing over the above cases we get (
15
).

•

For (
16
), the permutations counted by the left-hand side correspond to the insertion of
n
in any slot other than the first and last. In each case, the number of occurrences of both patterns remains unchanged. Thus, we get the right-hand side of (
16
).

The initial conditions are given by
T^{(1)}_{2,0,0}=T^{(2)}_{2,0,0}=1
,
T^{(1)}_{3,0,1}=T^{(2)}_{3,1,0}=1
,
T^{(1)}_{3,1,0}=T^{(2)}_{3,0,1}=0
,
T^{(3)}_{3,0,1}=T^{(3)}_{3,1,0}=0
, and
T^{(3)}_{3,1,0}=T^{(3)}_{3,0,1}=0
.
Thus, by using induction on
n
,
k
and
\ell
, we complete the proof.
∎

Remark
.

The number
T_{n,0,0}
in Theorem
6
appears in OEIS
[
16
, A054091]
.

(Nr. A33 to A36). Moreover, all the pairs satisfy the recurrence relation

\displaystyle T_{n,k,\ell}=(n-2)T_{n-1,k,\ell}+T_{n-1,k-1,\ell}+T_{n-1,k,\ell-1}+T_{n-2,k,\ell}-T_{n-2,k-1,\ell-1},\hskip 8.5359ptn\geq 4,

(17)

with the initial conditions
T_{2,0,0}=2
,
T_{3,0,0}=4
,
T_{3,1,0}=T_{3,0,1}=1
,
and
T_{n,k,\ell}=0
for
k,\ell\geq n-1
.
Equivalently,

\displaystyle T_{n}(x,y)=(n+x+y-2)T_{n-1}(x,y)+(1-xy)T_{n-2}(x,y),\hskip 8.5359ptn\geq 4,

(18)

with the initial conditions
T_{2}(x,y)=2
and
T_{3}(x,y)=x+y+4
.

Proof.

\lxSVG@closescope

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{

}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.07 0.07 M 0.07 6.89 L 27.49 6.89 M 0.07 13.78 L 27.49 13.78 M 0.07 20.67 L 27.49 20.67 M 6.89 0.07 L 6.89 27.49 M 13.78 0.07 L 13.78 27.49 M 20.67 0.07 L 20.67 27.49 M 27.49 27.49}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.89 20.67 M 8.34 20.67 C 8.34 21.47 7.69 22.12 6.89 22.12 C 6.09 22.12 5.44 21.47 5.44 20.67 C 5.44 19.87 6.09 19.22 6.89 19.22 C 7.69 19.22 8.34 19.87 8.34 20.67 Z M 6.89 20.67}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 13.78 13.78 M 15.23 13.78 C 15.23 14.58 14.58 15.23 13.78 15.23 C 12.98 15.23 12.33 14.58 12.33 13.78 C 12.33 12.98 12.98 12.33 13.78 12.33 C 14.58 12.33 15.23 12.98 15.23 13.78 Z M 13.78 13.78}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 20.67 6.89 M 22.12 6.89 C 22.12 7.69 21.47 8.34 20.67 8.34 C 19.87 8.34 19.22 7.69 19.22 6.89 C 19.22 6.09 19.87 5.44 20.67 5.44 C 21.47 5.44 22.12 6.09 22.12 6.89 Z M 20.67 6.89}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}.

The initial conditions follow directly from the definition. We shall prove that for
n\geq 4

\displaystyle T^{(1)}_{n,k,\ell}

\displaystyle=T^{(1)}_{n-1,k,\ell-1}+T^{(2)}_{n-1,k,\ell}+T^{(3)}_{n-1,k,\ell},

(19)

\displaystyle T^{(2)}_{n,k,\ell}

\displaystyle=T^{(1)}_{n-1,k,\ell}+T^{(2)}_{n-1,k-1,\ell}+T^{(3)}_{n-1,k-1,\ell},

(20)

\displaystyle T^{(3)}_{n,k,\ell}

\displaystyle=(n-2)T_{n-1,k,\ell}+T^{(3)}_{n-1,k,\ell-1}-T^{(3)}_{n-1,k,\ell}.

(21)

For each
n
-permutation
\pi=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n}
counted by the left-hand side of each equation, we obtain it by inserting the largest element
n
into an
(n-1)
-permutation
\pi^{\prime}=\pi^{\prime}_{1}\pi^{\prime}_{2}\cdots\pi^{\prime}_{n-1}
.

•

For (
19
), in each case,
n
is inserted into the first slot. In the situation where
\pi^{\prime}_{1}=n-1
, an extra occurrence of
q_{2}
would be created, in the form of
n(n-1)\pi_{n}
. Thus, this case gives
T^{(1)}_{n-1,k,\ell-1}
. The two situations where
\pi^{\prime}_{n-1}=n-1
or
\pi^{\prime}_{i}=n-1
preserve the number of both patterns contributing to
T^{(2)}_{n-1,k,\ell}
and
T^{(3)}_{n-1,k,\ell}
, respectively.

•

For (
20
),
n
is inserted into the last slot in each case. First, when
\pi^{\prime}_{1}=n-1
, the insertion maintains the number of occurrences of both patterns, which gives
T^{(1)}_{n-1,k,\ell}
. Other situations would result in an extra occurrence of
q_{1}
, which is formed by
\pi_{j}\pi_{j+1}n
, where
\pi_{j+1}=n-1
. Thus, these cases together contribute to
T^{(2)}_{n-1,k-1,\ell}+T^{(3)}_{n-1,k-1,\ell}
. Summing over the above cases we get the equation.

•

For (
21
), the permutations counted by the left-hand side correspond to the insertion of
n
in any slot other than the first and last. The situations where
\pi^{\prime}_{1}=n-1
and
\pi^{\prime}_{n-1}=n-1
maintain the number of occurrences of both patterns. Thus, these cases contribute to
(n-2)T^{(1)}_{n-1,k,\ell}
and
(n-2)T^{(2)}_{n-1,k,\ell}
, respectively. In the case where
\pi^{\prime}_{i}=n-1
, inserting
n
immediately to the left of
\pi^{\prime}_{i}
leads to an occurrence of
q_{2}
, formed by
n(n-1)\pi_{n}
contributing to
T^{(3)}_{n-1,k,\ell-1}
. In any other slot, the number of both patterns remains unchanged, resulting in
(n-3)T^{(3)}_{n-1,k,\ell}
. Summing over the above cases and using (
13
), we obtain the right side.

Let
H_{n,k,\ell}=T^{(1)}_{n,k-1,\ell}+T^{(2)}_{n,k,\ell-1}+T^{(3)}_{n,k,\ell}
.
By using (
19
), (
20
), and (
21
), we have

\displaystyle T_{n,k,\ell}=

\displaystyle\,(n-1)T_{n-1,k,\ell}+T_{n-1,k-1,\ell}+T_{n-1,k,\ell-1}-H_{n-1,k,\ell},

and

\displaystyle H_{n,k,\ell}

\displaystyle=\,(n-2)T_{n-1,k,\ell}+T_{n-1,k-1,\ell-1}+T_{n-1,k-1,\ell}+T_{n-1,k,\ell-1}-H_{n-1,k,\ell}

\displaystyle=\,T_{n,k,\ell}-T_{n-1,k,\ell}+T_{n-1,k-1,\ell-1}.

Combining the two equations, we obtain (
17
). Thus, (
18
) is derived from (
17
) as follows:

\displaystyle T_{n}(x,y)=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-2}\left((n-2)T_{n-1,k,\ell}+T_{n-1,k-1,\ell}+T_{n-1,k,\ell-1}\right)x^{k}y^{\ell}+\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-2}\left(T_{n-2,k,\ell}-T_{n-2,k-1,\ell-1}\right)x^{k}y^{\ell}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n-2)\sum_{k=0}^{n-3}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-3}T_{n-1,k,\ell}x^{k}y^{\ell}+x\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-3}T_{n-1,k-1,\ell}x^{k-1}y^{\ell}+y\sum_{k=0}^{n-3}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-2}T_{n-1,k,\ell-1}x^{k}y^{\ell-1}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=0}^{n-4}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-4}T_{n-2,k,\ell}x^{k}y^{\ell}-xy\sum_{k=0}^{n-3}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-3}T_{n-2,k-1,\ell-1}x^{k-1}y^{\ell-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(n+x+y-2\right)T_{n-1}(x,y)+(1-xy)T_{n-2}(x,y).

Hence, we complete the proof.
∎

It should be noted that all the patterns presented in Theorems
6
and
7
exhibit the same equidistribution. Let
T_{n,k}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-2}T_{n,k,\ell}
and
T_{n}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}T_{n,k}x^{k}
.

Corollary 8
.

All the patterns in Theorem
6
and
7
have the same equidistribution, and their enumeration satisfies the recurrence relation

\displaystyle T_{n,k}

\displaystyle=(n-1)T_{n-1,k}+T_{n-1,k-1}+T_{n-2,k}-T_{n-2,k-1},\hskip 8.5359ptn\geq 4,

(22)

with the initial conditions
T_{2,0}=2
and
T_{3,0}=5
.
Equivalently,

\displaystyle T_{n}(x)=(n+x-1)T_{n-1}(x)+(1-x)T_{n-2}(x),\hskip 8.5359ptn\geq 4,

(23)

with the initial conditions
T_{2}(x)=2
and
T_{3}(x)=x+5
.

Proof.

It is easy to obtain the initial conditions. For patterns in Theorem
6
, we have
T^{(1)}_{n,k}=T_{n-1,k}
since in the case where
\pi_{1}=n
removing
\pi_{1}
does not affect any occurrence of
q_{1}
.
We obtain (
22
) from (
14
), (
15
), and (
16
) as follows:

\displaystyle T_{n,k}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-2}(T^{(1)}_{n,k,\ell}+T^{(2)}_{n,k,\ell}+T^{(3)}_{n,k,\ell})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ T^{(1)}_{n-1,k}+T^{(2)}_{n-1,k}+T^{(3)}_{n-1,k}+T^{(1)}_{n-1,k}+T^{(2)}_{n-1,k-1}+T^{(3)}_{n-1,k-1}+(n-2)T_{n-1,k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ T_{n-1,k}+T_{n-2,k}+T_{n-1,k-1}-T_{n-2,k-1}+(n-2)T_{n-1,k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (n-1)T_{n-1,k}+T_{n-1,k-1}+T_{n-2,k}-T_{n-2,k-1}.

Moreover, (
23
) can be directly derived from (
22
) as follows:

\displaystyle T_{n}(x)=

\displaystyle(n-1)\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}T_{n-1,k}x^{k}+\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}T_{n-1,k-1}x^{k}+\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}T_{n-2,k}x^{k}-\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}T_{n-2,k-1}x^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n-1)T_{n-1}(x)+x\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}T_{n-1,k-1}x^{k-1}+T_{n-2}(x)-x\sum_{k=0}^{n-3}T_{n-2,k-1}x^{k-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n+x-1)T_{n-1}(x)+(1-x)T_{n-2}(x).

For patterns in Theorem
7
, by setting
y=1
in (
18
), we obtain (
23
).
By taking the coefficients of both sides of (
23
),
we get (
22
).
∎

Remark
.

The number
T_{n,0}
in Corollary
6
, which appears in OEIS
[
16
, A102038]
, coincides with
|I_{n}(0\underline{11})|
, the number of inversion sequences of length
n
avoiding the vincular pattern
0\underline{11}
.
This can be seen from (
22
) and the recurrence relation of
|I_{n}(0\underline{11})|
obtained by Lin and Yan
[
14
, Theorem 2.3]
, see also
[
1
, Table 2]
.

Finally, we connect
T_{n,k}
to the enumeration of pattern-avoiding inversion sequences.
The study of pattern avoidance in inversion sequences was initiated independently by Corteel
et al.

[
5
]
and by Mansour and Shattuck
[
8
]
.
An
inversion sequence of length

n
is an integer sequence
(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{n})
satisfying
0\leq e_{i}\leq i-1
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.
Let
\mathcal{I}(n,k)
denote the set of inversion sequences of length
n
that contain exactly
k
occurrences of the vincular pattern
\underline{00}
(i.e., consecutive equal entries), and define
I_{n,k}=|\mathcal{I}(n,k)|
.

Theorem 9
.

We have
I_{n,k}=T_{n,k}
for any
n,k
.

Proof.

By definition, we have
I_{n,0}=|I_{n}(0\underline{11})|
. The initial condition gives
I_{3,1}=T_{3,1}=1
.
For any
e=(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{n})
counted by
I_{n,k}
, we distinguish three cases.

•

e_{n}\neq e_{n-1}
. Then, for a fixed
(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{n-1})\in\mathcal{I}(n-1,k)
, there are exactly
n-1
choices for
e_{n}
. Thus, this case gives
(n-1)I_{n-1,k}
.

•

e_{n}=e_{n-1}=0
. In this case, there are
I_{n-2,k}
such inversion sequences.

•

e_{n}=e_{n-1}\neq 0
. Then
(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{n-1})
contains
k-1
occurrences of such pattern. Combining with the count for
e_{n}=e_{n-1}=0
, this case contributes to
I_{n-1,k-1}-I_{n-2,k-1}
.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle I_{n,k}

\displaystyle=(n-1)I_{n-1,k}+I_{n-1,k-1}+I_{n-2,k}-I_{n-2,k-1},

(24)

which coincides with the number
T_{n,k}
as seen in (
22
). This completes the proof.
∎
